# A spill slot past the scaled imm12

## Documentation

AArch64's `LDR`/`STR` unsigned-offset form does not carry a byte displacement. It carries a
**12-bit count of ACCESS UNITS**, so a 64-bit spill slot reaches `4095 * 8 = 32760` bytes off
the frame pointer and not one byte further. `arm64SlotDisplacement` is `16 + slot * 8`, so
**slot 4094 is the first one with no form** — `16 + 4094 * 8 = 32768`, whose scaled count is
`4096`, one past the field.

x64 has no such ceiling in any reachable range: `[rsp + disp32]` takes a full 32-bit signed
byte displacement directly, so `slotDisplacement` is a plain `Imm32` and the only limit it
acknowledges is `i32.max`. wasm addresses locals by index and has no frame at all. **The
ceiling is one target's, and it is the emitter that has to know it.**

⚠⚠ **x64 HAS NO ADDRESSING CEILING AND A FRAME CEILING ANYWAY, AND THE TWO ARE NOT THE SAME
QUESTION.** Reaching a slot is free there; OPENING the frame is not. Windows commits a thread
stack one page at a time and only when the GUARD PAGE ITSELF is touched, so a `sub rsp, 32808`
carries rsp eight pages past that page without ever accessing it, and the first write below lands
on reserved-but-uncommitted memory: `0xC0000005`, at a frame size the arm64 ceiling happens to sit
near for a completely unrelated reason. The prologue therefore WALKS the pages — `sub rsp, 4096` /
touch / repeat — for any frame past one page. ⇒ **these two cases exercise both ceilings at once**:
the arm64 imm12 displacement handling covers one target's, and the walk covers the other's. See
`X64Backend.encodeStackProbe`.

**THE THRESHOLD IS "MORE THAN ONE PAGE", AND THE EVIDENCE THAT FIXES IT IS A CASE THAT PASSES.**
An emitted PE's optional header has `SizeOfStackReserve` = 1,048,576 (1 MB) and
`SizeOfStackCommit` = **4096 — exactly one page**. A thread therefore starts with ONE committed
page and a guard page directly below it, and the guard services exactly ONE page of growth per
fault. So the question a frame has to answer is not "how big am I" but **"do I LAND ON the guard
page or step over it"**:

  • `x64-large-frame-arg7`'s frame is **6408 bytes, 1.56 pages** — and it is GREEN without a walk.
    It lands one page below the committed region, which is the guard page itself: the fault fires,
    the handler commits, execution resumes.
  • These cases' frame is **32808 bytes, 8.01 pages** — it lands in the NINTH page, far below the
    guard, in reserved address space with nothing watching it.

⚠ **A 1.56-page frame is not UNCONDITIONALLY safe, and that is exactly why the threshold is one
page rather than "whatever has passed so far".** Its safety depends on `rsp` sitting near the TOP
of its committed page; the same function called with `rsp` low in that page reaches past the guard
and faults. The only bound that holds wherever rsp sits is one page — a frame of exactly 4096
reaches `rsp - 4096`, which is inside the guard page for every possible rsp — so the comparison is
**strictly greater than 4096**, and a frame of exactly one page emits no walk at all. The case
below pins the other side of that line: 4104 bytes, the smallest frame that must walk.

⛔⛔ **AND THE WALK MAY NOT TOUCH ONE BYTE BELOW THE FRAME BASE — A SEPARATE OBLIGATION FROM COVERING
EVERY PAGE, AND THE ONE A GREEN THREAD COLLECTS ON.** A green thread's stack is an exact `mmap`'d region.
Its prologue guard projects `rsp - frameBytes - margin` against a guard word the grower placed
(`GtRuntime.gtStackGuardHeight`), so the room below a frame's base is the margin and nothing more — where
an OS-grown stack has pages and pages. A walk that TOUCHED and then compared would overshoot the base by
`4096 - (frameBytes mod 4096)` bytes, up to a whole page, and on a green thread that write lands off the
bottom of the stack.

⚠ **THE COMPILER ITSELF IS SUCH A PROGRAM.** The called-once inliner splices every function `main` alone
calls into it, which carries the compiler's own `main` to a frame over 12 KB, opened on a green thread a
few KB above the lowest page of its stack — legal, and what the guard reserves. A touch-then-compare walk's
last touch lands BELOW that page, and `maxon version` SIGSEGVs on x64-linux, printing nothing at all.
⇒ **the walk COMPARES FIRST**: it stops
when the next touch would be at or below the base, lands on the base, and touches THAT. Every page is still
covered — consecutive touches are one page apart and the base is less than a page below the last of them.

⚠ **x64-windows CANNOT SHOW THIS AND IS NOT EVIDENCE ABOUT IT.** That lane reserves
`GtWindowsExceptionDispatchBytes` — two pages — under the guard word for the OS's exception dispatch, so a
page of overshoot fits inside a reservation that exists for an unrelated reason. **x64-linux is the lane
where this case's overshoot is a fault**, so a green result on another lane is not evidence that the walk
stops at the base. arm64's prologue probes a frame over one page the same way, comparing before each
touch (`specs/stack-guard-pages.md`).

⚠ **The cure the sibling ceilings took is not available here.** `wide-field-offsets.md`'s
struct-field displacement is a compile-time constant at instruction selection, so the ISel
folds it into the scratch base itself (`StdToArm64Conversion.addressFor`). A spill SLOT
number does not exist at ISel — it is minted by the splitter, inside register allocation,
which runs after. There is nothing for an ISel-tier fold to fold.

⚠ **And the scratch register cannot be x16.** The ISel mints `x16` producer/consumer pairs
that later passes demonstrably splice ops between; a spill or a reload is exactly such a
spliced op, so an encoder-tier `x16` materialisation for spill addressing would clobber a
pending fold. `x17` — the other AAPCS64 IP scratch, outside the allocatable pool, and used
only inside a single terminator (`arm64JumpTable`, which declares both) — is the one
register no post-ISel splice can be sitting inside.

⚠ **AND THE SPILL SLOT IS NOT THE ONLY FRAME REFERENCE THAT OUTRUNS THE FIELD.** An INCOMING
STACK ARGUMENT is read at `[x29 + frameSize - outgoingArgSize + slot*8]`, a displacement the
whole frame contributes to — so it passes the ceiling *sooner* than any spill slot does, and it
is `Arm64PrologueEpilogue` rather than the encoder that mints it (only that pass knows the frame
size). It folds the same way, into the same `x17`, through the same split.

## Tests

<!-- test: spill-slot-past-the-scaled-imm12 -->
`sink(3)` is `4`, so `v0`..`v4110` are `5`..`4115` and their sum is
`8468660`. Nothing about the arithmetic is the point: the point is
**4111 values live at one program point**, which forces the splitter to spill
4094 distinct values, whose highest slot is **4094** — the first with no
scaled-imm12 form. It is the smallest program that reaches it: 4109 values stop
one slot short and compile clean on every target.

```maxon
typealias Integer = int(i64.min to i64.max)

function sink(x Integer) returns Integer
	return try x + 1 otherwise panic("out of range")
end 'sink'

function main() returns ExitCode
	let n = sink(3)
	let v0 = try n + 1 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v1 = try n + 2 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v2 = try n + 3 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v3 = try n + 4 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v4 = try n + 5 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v5 = try n + 6 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v6 = try n + 7 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v7 = try n + 8 otherwise panic("out of range")
	let v8 = try n + 9 otherwise panic("out of range")
```

<!-- test: wide-incoming-argument-past-the-scaled-imm12 -->
`wide`'s ten arguments sum to `55`, so `v0`..`v4110` are `56`..`4166` and their
sum is `8678321`. The arithmetic is again not the point: the point is that
**`a8` and `a9` travel on the STACK** (AAPCS64 passes the first eight of a file in
registers), so `wide` reads them at `[x29 + frameSize - outgoingArgSize + slot*8]`
— and its 4111 live values put `frameSize` past the scaled field, so that
displacement has no direct form either. It is a SECOND door on the same ceiling,
in a second pass, and the first case cannot reach it: a function with eight
arguments or fewer reads none of them from the caller's frame.

```maxon

	let s = wide(1, a1: 2, a2: 3, a3: 4, a4: 5, a5: 6, a6: 7, a7: 8, a8: 9, a9: 10)
	return 0 if s == 8678321 else 1
end 'main'
```
```exitcode
0
```

<!-- test: the-smallest-frame-that-walks-the-guard-pages -->
`sink(3)` is `4`, so `v0`..`v521` are `5`..`526` and their sum is `138591`. The
arithmetic is once more not the point: **522 values is the SMALLEST count whose x64 frame
passes 4096**, and it passes it by 8 bytes — `4104`, one page plus a single word (521
values reserve `4088` and emit a bare `sub rsp`). It is therefore the tightest REMAINDER
the page walk can be handed, and the one place an off-by-one in it shows: `rsp-4096` is
inside the frame, `rsp-8192` is 4088 bytes below its base, and the walk must touch the
first and not the second. A walk that landed on its last PROBE rather than on the base
would run this function on a frame 4088 bytes below its own, and the sum would come back
wrong.

The 4094-slot cases above cannot reach this edge — their remainder is 40 bytes of a
32808-byte frame, eight pages in, where an off-by-one is one iteration among many.

```maxon

function sink(x Integer) returns Integer
	return try x + 1 otherwise panic("out of range")
end 'sink'
```

<!-- test: a-walking-frame-on-a-green-threads-stack -->
`wide` reserves the SAME 4104-byte frame as the case above and opens it on a GREEN THREAD's stack rather
than the process's: 64 spawns in turn, each on a seed stack the grower has to enlarge before the frame
clears the guard word, each answering `138591`. The room below that frame base is the guard margin and
nothing else, so a walk that overshot the base by the frame's 4088-byte remainder would write off the
bottom of the stack and SIGSEGV.

⚠ **ONE SPAWN IS NOT THE GATE, AND THAT IS WHY THERE ARE 64.** Whether the page under a given green
thread's stack happens to be mapped is the allocator's business: where it is, the overshoot silently
corrupts a neighbour instead of faulting, and the sum still comes back right, so a single spawn can
pass against an overshooting walk; 64 fresh stacks is what makes the fault the outcome.
```maxon
typealias Integer = int(i64.min to i64.max)

function sink(x Integer) returns Integer
	return try x + 1 otherwise panic("out of range")
end 'sink'

	let n = sink(3)

end 'wide'

function main() returns ExitCode
	var wrong = 0 as Integer
	var i = 0 as Integer
	while i < 64 'each'
		let p = async wide()
		let s = await p
		if s != 138591 'mismatch'
			wrong = try wrong + 1 otherwise panic("out of range")
		end 'mismatch'
		i = i + 1
	end 'each'
	return 0 if wrong == 0 else 1
end 'main'
```
